# Decision Log

> **Purpose**: The "why" file. An **append-only** log of every meaningful decision —
> which library was chosen and why, architecture choices, feature decisions, branch
> decisions, tradeoffs. When anyone (human or AI) wonders "why is it built this way?",
> the answer is here.
>
> **Update rule (MANDATORY)**: Append a new entry for EVERY meaningful decision.
> **Never edit or delete past entries** — that would rewrite history and break the
> log's purpose. Before making a new decision, check this log first (don't decide
> twice).

---

## What counts as a "meaningful decision"? (MANDATORY — log all of these)

- **Library / framework / tool choice** — component library, icon set, state manager, animation lib, styling approach
- **Architecture / pattern choice** — folder structure, data flow, error strategy, server vs client components
- **Feature design decisions** — scope, UX, API shape, data model
- **Branch / workflow decisions** — git flow, release process, deployment target
- **Anything you had to think about for more than ~5 seconds**

---

## How to add a decision

1. Copy the **Template** below into the **Decision Entries** section (newest on top)
2. Fill it in — the **Why** line is the most important part
3. Add a row to the **Decision Index** table
4. If it supersedes an earlier decision, mark the old one as `Superseded by ADR-NNN`

---

## Template

### ADR-NNN: [Short title]
- **Date**: YYYY-MM-DD
- **Status**: Proposed | Accepted | Rejected | Superseded by ADR-NNN
- **Context**: [what triggered this decision — the problem being solved]
- **Options considered**: [alternatives, and why each was rejected]
- **Decision**: [what was chosen]
- **Why**: [the reasoning — this is the important part. Write enough that a future agent
  understands without re-deriving it.]
- **Consequences**: [positive and negative effects, things to watch out for]
- **Affects**: [features / files / branches this touches]

## Decision Entries

### ADR-144: Phase 2 P1 hot paths - schema cache, private admin reads, edge login, client cache (2026-10-26)

- **Date**: 2026-10-26
- **Status**: **Accepted (implemented locally; staging deploy and production gate pending)**. Resolves the Phase 2 scope questions recorded in the spec. Production remains untouched.
- **Context**: Phase 0 measured the dominant read/auth waste at A/B: order detail 7 statements + a stops `PRAGMA`, vendor route 8 statements (2 `PRAGMA`s), a subscription `PRAGMA` on every call, 3–4 full-table admin aggregates per dashboard poll, login 4 statements / 2 round trips before user lookup using the Cloudflare edge socket as the IP key, an unbounded per-isolate anon-throttle map, and a Flutter client that retried 429s and cached for only 45 s. The Free-plan 10 ms CPU ceiling still blocks a meaningful A/B/C benchmark, so this phase cuts waste rather than certifying capacity.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Cache migration-static schema metadata per isolate** in `app/db_schema.py` (`stop_columns`, `order_columns`). Cache only on a successful probe; a failed probe returns an empty set and retries. Never cache request or user data.
  2. **Fold order-detail rider + stops-ahead into one join** (`stops`/`routes`/`users`/`vendor_profile`), preserving the broad tolerant `except` and the response shape. Worst case 4 statements.
  3. **Cache `_ensure_billing_mode` behind `_billing_ready`**: probe → ALTER missing 026/028 columns → re-probe once → ready. A swallowed ALTER error cannot mark a broken schema ready.
  4. **Admin reads are private**: `cached(private=True)` emits `Cache-Control: private, no-store` with no ETag/CDN header. `/admin/metrics` and `/admin/metrics/overview` add a 60 s per-isolate TTL cache keyed by `"metrics"` / `"overview:<days>"`; only global aggregates are stored, and `days` 1–90 bounds the map at 91 entries.
  5. **Bound `/v1/windows`**: past dates clamp to today; dates beyond today+30 return 400 `VALIDATION` (the project's `ValidationError` mapping), bounding cache cardinality at 31 input dates.
  6. **Login uses `CF-Connecting-IP`** (fallback: socket peer; never logged), raises the IP ceiling 20/hr → 100/hr, keeps phone 5/15 min, and combines both checks into one D1 batch (multi-row UPSERT + `IN (...)` SELECT). L1 still denies first; D1 counting stays fail-open.
  7. **Bound the anon throttle** at 2,048 keys by pruning expired entries and evicting oldest insertions.
  8. **Flutter public reads**: 300 s fresh / 20 min stale, GET 429 never retried, cacheable GETs attempt once and serve stale on 429/5xx/transport failure, `clearReadCache()` at the auth boundary, and no `startTracking` after the tracking screen is disposed.
  9. **Vendor sync ships the PRAGMA-only slice.** Full chunked batching is deferred because D1 `batch()` is all-or-nothing; batching multiple items would turn one bad stop into a whole-chunk rollback.
- **Why**: Every change removes work that repeats per request without altering a money path, a CAS guard, a response shape, or the schema. The schema cache is safe because migrations are static for an isolate's lifetime. The admin cache is safe because it holds only single-tenant global aggregates and no money decision reads them. The 100/hr IP cap keeps shared NAT usable while the per-phone cap remains the primary credential-stuffing guard. The client TTL now matches the server's `Cache-Control`.
- **Consequences**: Steady-state costs drop to 4 statements for order detail, 6 / 0 probes for the vendor route, 0 probes after the first subscription/vendor call, 2 statements / 1 round trip for login checks, ≤1 admin aggregate pass per 60 s, and a bounded throttle map. Admin analytics may be up to 60 s stale. Admin responses can no longer enter a shared cache. A client on an old build still works; the server changes are backward compatible. No migration means rollback is a code revert, slice by slice.
- **Verification**: Backend **459 passed in 15.03 s**; ruff clean on every changed file (43 pre-existing repo-wide errors remain in untouched files). Flutter **75 passed**; `flutter analyze` no issues. New tests: 16 backend (`tests/test_phase2_hot_paths.py`) covering statement counts, rider parity, probe-once caches, legacy ALTERs, private headers, metrics TTL/expiry, windows bounds, CF-IP, one-batch login, phone limit, throttle bounds; 8 Flutter (`test/api_client_cache_test.dart`) covering TTL hits, cache clear, single-attempt 429, immediate stale fallback, and the tracking-dispose guard. Existing vendor query-count test adjusted to warm the cache and assert steady state.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/{db_schema.py,repositories/order_repo.py,services/{order_service,vendor_service,subscription_service,auth_service}.py,api/{caching.py,middleware.py,v1/{admin,catalog,auth}.py}}`, `workers/api/{conftest.py,tests/{test_phase2_hot_paths.py,test_vendor.py}}`, `apps/user_app_donor/{lib/{core/api_client.dart,main.dart,features/orders/tracking_screen.dart},test/api_client_cache_test.dart}`, `Feature_docs/performance/{2026-10-26-phase2-p1-hot-paths-spec.md,2026-10-26-phase2-results.md}`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`.

### ADR-143: Phase 1A/1B staging verification - migration 029, cron-off deploy, synthetic atomicity smoke (2026-10-26)

- **Date**: 2026-10-26
- **Status**: **Accepted (staging verified)** - migration 029 applied to `shodasha-staging`, `water-staging` deployed with cron disabled, synthetic atomicity smoke passed. Production remains untouched; production migration/deploy requires a separate explicit owner gate. Resolves the staging-blocked status recorded in ADR-141/142.
- **Context**: The 2026-10-25 staging apply was rejected by the D1 free-tier daily row-write limit (`7500`). The quota reset at 00:00 UTC; the retry was the first action on 2026-10-26. Phase 1A/1B code was already committed locally (`bfac196`, `4f10070`) and green on SQLite. The smoke needed to exercise the deployed Worker and real D1 without real money or provider calls.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Apply 029 additively** - one migration run (19 commands); verify 28 migrations, every new column/table, and all 9 indexes before deploying.
  2. **Deploy with cron off** - `water-staging` `crons: []`, code version `474a9a1a-11e1-4cc9-8a97-6bf0964689fe`; the staging-only `UPI_WEBHOOK_SECRET` upload produced active version `4ee48994-af50-4116-82ec-8a544d1a4b6b` (same code). No `UPI_KEY_ID`/`UPI_KEY_SECRET`, so no provider intent path was used.
  3. **Synthetic smoke** - six `smoke-20261026053456-*` orders, four payments, one dues intent, one stop; seeded `load-user-0003` Bearer token; HMAC-signed webhook bodies; direct D1 rows for setup; no real Razorpay calls. Scenarios: cancel twice, duplicate webhook, dues settled twice, reissue four times, COD after a capture attempt, COD switch on an unpaid link.
  4. **Assert DB invariants after each scenario** - one refund, one paid payment, one dues allocation, 3 reissue rows/3 audits then 429, captured payment blocks COD, one 2800 dues event and idempotent replay.
- **Why**: This is the smallest safe way to prove the CAS batches on real D1 without touching production or moving real money. Using the seeded token and direct synthetic rows keeps auth real while making every money row disposable and inspectable. Cron stays off so the smoke cannot create subscription orders or interfere with the assertions.
- **Consequences**: Phase 1A/1B are staging-verified; the production gate is now the only remaining deployment step. The Free-plan 10 ms CPU ceiling still prevents meaningful A/B/C staging runs and remains a launch blocker. Staging now contains the synthetic `smoke-20261026053456-*` rows (no production impact); the staging-only webhook secret lives outside the repo. The smoke changed no code or production data.
- **Verification**: 28 migrations; all 9 indexes; 1,021 users / 10,000 orders / 0 open dues intents; `/health` 200 exact + `no-store`; `/v1/catalog` 200; seeded user/vendor tokens 200. Smoke statuses: cancel 200 → 409 `ALREADY_CANCELLED`; webhook 200 → 200 `duplicate:true`; dues 200 → 200 `duplicate:true`; reissue 200/200/200 → 429 `RATE_LIMITED`; COD capture guard 409 `STATE_CONFLICT`; COD switch 200 + replay 200. No 5xx; no secrets printed.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/db/migrations/029_p0_correctness.sql`, staging Worker `water-staging`, staging D1 `shodasha-staging`, `Feature_docs/performance/2026-10-25-phase1-results.md` (§5.1/§10), `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`.

### ADR-142: Phase 1B read-path N+1 batching - two bulk aggregates per path (2026-10-25)

- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: **Accepted (Phase 1B only)** — implemented and verified locally on 2026-10-25; committed locally, not pushed. Staging verification of Phase 1A and 1B is still blocked by the D1 free-tier daily row-write limit (`7500`) until 2026-10-26 00:00 UTC; production is untouched.
- **Context**: Phase 0 measured `GET /v1/vendor/routes/today` at **85 statements / 162 rows** for 40 stops and `GET /v1/billing/dues` at **15 / 14** for ~6 open orders. In both paths the service loop called `LedgerRepo.get` + `PaymentRepo.paid_sum_for_order` per stop, or `paid_sum_for_order` + `_dues_posted` per open order, so the statement count grew linearly with the result set. Phase 1A (ADR-141) deliberately deferred this work so a read-path change could not be confused with a money regression.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Keep the per-row reads** — rejected: 80 of the route statements and 11–12 of the dues statements are the loop, and every statement is a sequential D1 roundtrip.
  2. **Use D1 `batch()` for the aggregate reads** — rejected for this slice: 4–6 sequential reads already hit the target, and `batch()` adds result-shape and failure-mode complexity without a measured need.
  3. **Cache `_stop_field_cols` pragma probes in the same change** — rejected: those are two fixed probes, not N+1, and caching introduces schema-invalidation risk. Separate slice.
  4. **Chunked `IN (...)` aggregates (chosen)** — one statement per aggregate per 90 ids, exact predicates copied from the single-row helpers, minimal diff.
- **Decision**:
  1. **`LedgerRepo.held_for_customers(ids)`** — `SELECT customer_id, held FROM ledger WHERE customer_id IN (?,...)`, chunk 90, missing customer → `0`.
  2. **`PaymentRepo.paid_sums_for_orders(ids)`** — `SELECT order_id, COALESCE(SUM(amount),0) ... WHERE order_id IN (?,...) AND status IN ('paid','partial') GROUP BY order_id`, chunk 90, missing order → `0`.
  3. **`PaymentRepo.dues_posted_for_orders(ids)`** — `SELECT DISTINCT ref_id FROM ledger_events WHERE ref_type='order' AND kind='dues' AND d_dues>0 AND ref_id IN (?,...)`, chunk 90, returns a set.
  4. **`today_route`** — dedupe non-null customer/order ids, call both aggregates once, then map `held`/`paid_sum` into the existing stop loop. Address-snapshot overlay and the `held > 3` hold-block rule are unchanged.
  5. **`get_dues`** — call both aggregates once, then compute `paid`, `due`, `order_due`, and `lines` in the existing order. The COD double-count guard (`order_due` excludes orders with a posted dues event) is preserved.
  6. **Safety** — ids are deduped and empty strings dropped; an empty list skips the query (no `IN ()`); 90 keeps every statement under D1's 100-bound-parameter cap.
  7. **Not changed** — `paid_sum_for_order`, `_dues_posted`, `get_invoice`, `cod_confirm`, `settle_dues_intent`, admin/order-detail N+1, `_stop_field_cols`, any migration, any write path, any API shape.
- **Why**: These are the two largest read-path N+1s and both sit on the morning-busy surfaces (vendor route, customer dues). Copying the exact predicates of the proven single-row helpers keeps behavior identical while removing 80 and 11 sequential roundtrips. A 90-id chunk is a deliberate margin below D1's 100-parameter cap, and skipping empty lists avoids a malformed `IN ()`. Keeping single-order helpers intact means the money-sensitive callers (`get_invoice`, `cod_confirm`) are untouched.
- **Consequences**: A ≤90-stop route is now 6 queries/6 roundtrips at service level (route + 2 pragma probes + stops + 2 aggregates) and dues is 4/4 for ≤90 open orders; each extra 90 ids adds one statement per aggregate. Rows read stay roughly flat (route 42, dues 40) — the win is latency, not row volume. An empty id list skips both aggregates. Local SQLite still does not model D1 network latency or row accounting, so staging remains the proof. Phase 1B does not address the free-plan 10 ms CPU ceiling.
- **Verification**: Targeted `test_vendor.py` + `test_payments.py` = **54 passed**; full backend = **443 passed in 23.00s**; ruff clean on all changed source/test files. New tests: per-customer/per-order map parity, query count equal for 1 vs 40 stops, 95-customer chunk/dedupe, dues query count equal for 1 vs 40 open orders, and a 95-order chunk test asserting exactly 6 dues statements (1 ledger + 1 orders + 2 paid chunks + 2 posted chunks). Observability metrics measured route **6/6/42** and dues **4/4/40** (queries/roundtrips/rows).
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/repositories/{ledger_repo,payment_repo}.py`, `workers/api/src/app/services/{vendor_service,payment_service}.py`, `workers/api/tests/{test_vendor,test_payments}.py`, `Feature_docs/performance/2026-10-25-phase1-results.md`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-141: Phase 1 P0 correctness design - one D1 batch per money mutation, SQL CAS guards (2026-10-25)

- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: **Accepted (Phase 1A only)** — implemented and verified locally on 2026-10-25; staging apply is blocked by the D1 free-tier daily row-write limit (`7500`) until 2026-10-26 00:00 UTC; Phase 1B is deferred; production is untouched. Evidence: `Feature_docs/performance/2026-10-25-phase1-results.md`.
- **Context**: Phase 0 proved A/B are throughput-feasible on Workers Paid but unsafe until the audit's P0 money/cron defects are fixed. `WRITE_LOCK` serializes local SQLite but is a no-op on Workers, and `D1Conn.commit()`/`rollback()` are no-ops, so every money invariant currently relies on sequential statements that can partially apply. D1 `batch()` is a real SQL transaction and counts as one query against the 1,000-query invocation cap (100-bound-parameter limit per statement). The read-only design pass covered cancel, webhook, dues, transitions, subscription cancel, COD switch, reissue, cron, purge, and hot filters.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Keep sequential writes and rely on `WRITE_LOCK`** — rejected: no-op across Worker isolates; a mid-way failure leaves a cancelled order without refund/ledger rows and retries cannot repair it.
  2. **Add a Durable Object per order/user to serialize** — rejected for this phase: new binding/runtime dependency and cost; SQL CAS gives the same single-writer guarantee with one new table where needed.
  3. **One `batch()` per mutation with compare-and-set guards (chosen)** — atomic, testable on SQLite with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, and keeps the existing repositories/services.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Cancel (P0-1)** — one batch; first statement `UPDATE orders ... WHERE id=? AND state=?` sets `mutation_token`. Every later statement is guarded by the token, so a loser writes nothing. Real-money payments are claimed with `payments.refund_id IS NULL`; credit rows are restored before the balance is recomputed from `SUM(subscription_credit_entries.delta)`.
  2. **Webhook (P0-2)** — payment CAS `WHERE id=? AND status!='paid'` sets `settlement_token`; a SQL-branched order update marks paid only when the order is live and non-COD, otherwise the capture queues exactly one refund. `payments.refund_id` is the one-refund-path marker; a late capture creates its own refund row.
  3. **Dues (P0-3)** — intent CAS sets `settlement_token`; allocations are inserted per open order with guards; the refund remainder is derived in SQL from rows actually written, never from a pre-read total. A unique partial index allows one open intent per user.
  4. **Transition (P0-4)** — order CAS + event + postpaid dues in one batch; loser gets 409, no duplicate event/dues.
  5. **Subscription cancel (P0-5)** — `canceled_at IS NULL` CAS; returns/credit/refund rows first, balance zeroed last; failure rolls back the whole batch.
  6. **COD switch (P0-6)** — conditional CAS rejects captured/paid orders and fails open links; a UPI capture after conversion is refunded by the webhook COD guard.
  7. **Reissue (P0-7)** — new `delivery_code_reissues` table with PK `(order_id, seq)` and a sliding 24h count; bounded retry on sequence conflict; no Durable Object.
  8. **Cron (P0-8/P0-9)** — `process_due` stops advancing `next_run`; `run_due_subscriptions` creates the order and settles prepaid credit first, then advances via `next_run` CAS. Failures increment `due_attempts` and set `due_retry_at` with 15m→24h exponential backoff. Page size `_DUE_LIMIT=60`, ordered by `next_run, id`; keep `*/15` cron.
  9. **Purge (P0-10)** — `_PURGE_CHUNK=90` for every `IN (...)` list (D1's 100-param cap).
  10. **Indexes (P0-11)** — migration 029 adds `ledger_events(ref_type,ref_id)`, `audit_log(created_at)`, `idempotency_keys(created_at)`, `rate_counters(window_start)`, `subscriptions(status,next_run)`, `orders(address_id,state)`, `payments(refund_id)`; each verified with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`.
  11. **Migration 029** — additive only: tokens, `refund_id`, `due_*`, reissues table, unique open dues intent (stale duplicates marked `failed`, no money moves), indexes. Old code ignores the new schema, so code rollback is safe.
  12. **Phase 1B** — vendor-route and dues N+1 batching is deferred to a separate commit/release after 1A verification.
- **Why**: The first A/B blocker is correctness, not throughput. A single batch makes every money mutation all-or-nothing on D1; a CAS makes concurrent duplicate callbacks, cancels, transitions, and reissues produce exactly one outcome. Deriving refunds from written rows closes the cancel-vs-webhook race without a distributed lock. Create-then-advance closes the silent missed-delivery class.
- **Consequences**: Adds one migration and a small reissue table; money paths become one roundtrip each but larger statements. Local tests must emulate D1 all-or-nothing (`BEGIN IMMEDIATE`) and a fake D1 binding must cover the failure path. The 60-sub page drains a 100-sub cluster in two ticks (≤30 min); cron delay is already tolerable. Free plan remains non-viable (10 ms CPU and the 50-query cron cap), and the D1 free-tier daily row-write cap (`7500`) blocks even the additive staging migration; both are launch blockers. Production migration/deploy still requires a separate explicit approval after staging verification.
- **Resolved decisions (2026-10-25)**: owner approved Phase 1A only and every recommendation — migration 029 as specified; `*/15` cron with 60 due subscriptions per tick; 15m→24h backoff with no dead-letter table; `delivery_code_reissues` instead of a Durable Object; `payments.refund_id` as the one-refund-path marker with late captures creating their own refund row; and a separate production gate.
- **Verification**: Local implementation complete: **438 passed** (400 before 1A), ruff clean, and `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` proves the named index for eight hot queries. New suites: 13 atomicity, 9 D1-batch, 9 index, 12 scheduler tests. Staging pre-flight (read-only): 27 migrations (latest `028`), 1,021 users, 10,000 orders, **0 open dues intents**. Applying 029 failed at 2026-10-25 11:44 UTC with Cloudflare code `7500` (D1 free-tier daily row-write limit); the DB is unchanged and no bypass was attempted. Artifacts: `Feature_docs/performance/results/phase1a-pytest.txt`, `phase1a-ruff.txt`.
- **Affects**: `Feature_docs/performance/{2026-10-25-phase1-p0-correctness-spec.md,2026-10-25-phase1-results.md,results/phase1a-*.txt}`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`; `workers/api/src/app/{db_d1.py,db/migrations/029_p0_correctness.sql,jobs/scheduler.py,repositories/{ledger_repo,order_repo,payment_repo}.py,services/{dispatch_service,order_service,payment_service,subscription_service}.py}`, `workers/api/tests/{test_p0_atomicity,test_d1_batch,test_indexes,test_scheduler}.py` plus migration-list updates across the suite; staging D1 `shodasha-staging` (blocked, unchanged), branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-140: Phase 0 safe measurement layer - staging, redacted logs, real health, guarded benchmark (2026-10-25)

- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: Accepted (implemented; staging deployed and measured; production untouched; Paid Workers required before A/B can be re-run)
- **Context**: The 1,000-user audit (ADR-139) said A/B are throughput-feasible on Paid but unsafe until P0 money/cron defects are fixed, and C needs architecture. It also said Free is not viable. The owner approved Phase 0 to make A/B measurable without touching production, on the Free plan, with no secrets in logs, and with status polling unchanged.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Load-test production** — rejected: 9 real users, no rollback for a money/cron race, and the audit’s contract forbids it.
  2. **Code-first optimization without measurement** — rejected: the audit already showed correctness, not raw throughput, is the first A/B blocker; guessing at latency would hide the platform ceiling.
  3. **Isolated staging + local query-shape baseline + redacted logs + guarded harness (chosen)** — staging proves the deployed path without production risk; local removes the Cloudflare CPU ceiling so the app’s own query shape can be measured; the harness refuses production.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Health** — `GET /health` runs `SELECT 1` through the normal DB dependency, 2 s timeout, 5 s per-isolate cache. Success stays exactly `{"status":"ok"}`; DB failure is 503 with additive `{"status":"degraded","checks":{"db":"error"}}`; both `Cache-Control: no-store`. No error text is returned.
  2. **Observability** — one JSON line per request on `shodasha.request`: `event, ts, env, trace_id, method, route, status, duration_ms, db_queries, db_roundtrips, db_rows_read, db_rows_written`. `route` is the FastAPI template, never the raw path. 4xx/5xx and slow requests always log; healthy fast paths sample deterministically. Authorization, cookies, tokens, phone numbers, UPI ids, provider refs, client IPs, query strings, bodies, and raw user paths are never logged.
  3. **Counters** — `D1Conn` reads `meta.rows_read`/`meta.rows_written`/`changes`; `batch` counts N statements and one roundtrip; `AsyncSqliteConn` counts local rows for the same schema. No double counting.
  4. **Trace safety** — `X-Trace-Id` must match `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,64}$`; malformed values are replaced, so a client cannot inject newlines or PII into logs.
  5. **Staging** — `env.staging` in `wrangler.jsonc`: Worker `water-staging`, D1 `shodasha-staging`, `APP_ENV=staging`, `OBS_LOG_SAMPLE_RATE=0.1`, `OBS_SLOW_MS=750`, `THROTTLE_ANON_PER_MIN=100000`, no cron, logs/traces persisted. No secrets in vars.
  6. **Seed and harness** — `scripts/seed_phase0.py` builds a deterministic 43,203-row dataset (1,021 users, 1,000 addresses/ledger/subscriptions, 10,000 orders, 24,000 events, 3,000 payments, 800 stops, 1,021 sessions, 100 audit rows) with true UPSERTs, because D1 runs `PRAGMA foreign_keys = 1`. `scripts/bench_phase0.py` is GET-only, constant-arrival A/B/C + smoke, refuses production, requires `--allow-stress` for C, aborts on >2% 5xx or p99 >5 s, and writes JSON + Markdown.
  7. **No business logic, schema, money path, or production data changed.**
- **Why**: A/B decisions need per-route evidence, not a synthetic guess. The staging Worker exercises the real D1 binding, Pyodide runtime, middleware, and auth path without production risk. Local SQLite isolates the application’s query shape from the Free-plan CPU ceiling. Redaction and the target guard make the measurement layer safe to keep deployed.
- **Consequences**:
  - **Free plan is a launch blocker, not a Phase 0 bug.** Staging A/B/C aborted at 27.3% / 16.7% / 55.6% 5xx. Failed bodies were Cloudflare `error code: 1102`; the tail shows a Pyodide `SystemError` at `initPyInstance`, and 1101s follow as the known stuck-isolate failure. Production’s old `/health` also returns intermittent 1102, so the ceiling predates this phase and affects the current 9 users. Workers Free is 10 ms CPU; this Python Worker cannot meet it. Paid’s 30 s default removes it.
  - **Warm vs cold.** One warm keep-alive connection: first `/health` 1962 ms, then 19/19 200 at 118–194 ms. Cold/isolate startup is 1.5–4 s. Paid fixes the CPU rejection, not the cold-start cost; keep-alive and traffic shape still matter.
  - **Local A/B prove application execution, not D1 throughput.** Local A passed 1,668 requests at 2.78 rps, 0 errors, p50 11.4 ms / p95 29.0 ms / p99 34.8 ms; local B passed 3,336 requests at 5.56 rps, 0 errors, p50 9.8 ms / p95 25.8 ms / p99 32.8 ms. Local A recorded 1,662 and local B 3,324 stale-socket reconnects because the threads idle ~7 s against uvicorn's 5 s keep-alive; all requests returned 200, and query counters are unaffected. The runbook now recommends `--timeout-keep-alive 75` for local runs.
  - **Query shape is now evidence.** `/vendor/routes/today` = 85 statements / 162 rows for 40 stops (over Free’s 50-query cap), `/billing/dues` = 15/14 for ~6 open orders, `/orders/{id}` = 5/24, `/auth/me` = 6/6, `/orders` = 2/11, `/catalog` = 0/0, `/health` = 1/1. Vendor route and dues are the first N+1 fixes.
  - **C's knee remains unmeasured.** Staging C failed at the CPU ceiling (55.6% 5xx at 7 rps), not at a concurrency knee. C stays a stress target for a later architecture phase, not a launch gate.
  - **Operational cost.** The staging seed wrote 43,203 rows but D1 reported 168,115 rows written including indexes, over Free’s 100k/day. Re-seeding needs explicit approval. The first staging deploy needed a second run to register the workers.dev route (10007); the runbook records it.
  - **Next**: owner upgrades to Paid when ready (not now); until then keep measurement to staging/local. After the upgrade, re-probe production `/health` and staging; fix the P0 cancel/webhook/transition/cron/purge defects; batch the vendor route and dues; page the cron; then re-run staging A/B. Do not load-test production.
- **Verification**: Full backend suite **400 passed**; 26 benchmark guard tests; staging `/health` 200 exact body with `no-store` and a sanitized trace id; live log line `env=staging`, `route=/health`, `db_queries=1`, `db_roundtrips=1`, no secrets; seeded staging routes 200 one at a time; production migration check read-only → “No migrations to apply!”. Results: `Feature_docs/performance/2026-10-25-phase0-results.md`, artifacts in `Feature_docs/performance/results/`.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/{src/app/{api/health.py,api/deps.py,core/observability.py,core/errors.py,db_d1.py,main.py,api/middleware.py},wrangler.jsonc,.env.example,scripts/{seed_phase0.py,bench_phase0.py},tests/{test_observability.py,test_bench_guard.py,test_health.py}}`, `Feature_docs/performance/`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, staging Worker `water-staging` + D1 `shodasha-staging`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-139: 1,000-user capacity audit - model A/B/C, fix P0 first, C needs architecture (2026-10-25)

- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: Accepted (read-only audit; implementation awaits owner approval)
- **Context**: The owner asked whether Shodasha can serve 1,000 users and where it breaks. Production has 9 users on Worker `water` + D1 `shodasha`, so no production load test or write was allowed. "1,000 users" was ambiguous: registered, daily active, or concurrent.
- **Options considered**: (A) 1,000 registered at ~5% peak concurrency; (B) 1,000 DAU at ~10% peak concurrency; (C) 1,000 truly concurrent. Also Free vs Paid Workers plan.
- **Decision**: Audit only, no code. Model all three with Little's Law (20 API requests per 6-min session, 2.5x burst, 4 D1 statements/request, 20% write requests x 1.5 rows written). Verdict: A (2.78 rps avg / 6.94 peak) and B (5.56 / 13.9) are throughput-feasible on Workers Paid but not safe until P0 money/cron defects are fixed; C (55.6 / 139; 222-556 D1 qps; 41.7 writes/s) is NO-GO without batching, async writes, read models, cursor cron, and sharding. Free is not viable: 10ms CPU cannot run PBKDF2-100k login, the 50-query cron cap fails at ~4-5 subscriptions, and the 100k rows-written/day allowance is exceeded at B.
- **Why**: Raw D1 throughput is not the first A/B bottleneck. The first bottlenecks are non-atomic cancel/webhook/transition writes, a 15-minute cron that processes every due subscription in one 1,000-query/30s-CPU invocation, and N+1 reads (order detail 7 queries, vendor route ~82 per 40 stops, admin full-table aggregates). Scaling without fixing these would ship money loss and missed deliveries, not just latency.
- **Consequences**: A/B can launch only after P0 fixes + cron paging + indexes + N+1/rate-limit work and a staging benchmark. C needs a separate architecture phase. The report lists 12 owner questions (scenario choice, plan tier, peak mix, cron delay tolerance, staging budget, observability, migration state, SLOs, FCM, retention).
- **Affects**: `Feature_docs/performance/2026-10-25-1000-user-capacity-audit.md`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, future migrations/worker config/staging/load tests.

### ADR-138: Customer-flow hardening — session resilience, COD fallback, replay handling, server money guards (2026-10-25)
- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: Accepted (implemented and verified locally on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; production untouched; not pushed; custom dates/twice-daily remain blocked on product answers)
- **Context**: A source-verified end-to-end audit of the Shodasha customer flow found four release-blocking classes of defects. (1) Session restore used a 5s timeout while `ApiClient.refreshSession` allowed 12s, and `recoverSession` treated every failure as a dead session, so a slow refresh or a transient network/429/5xx error wiped the stored pair and forced the customer to sign in again. (2) A post-create UPI failure left the order unpaid, but the only recovery options were "Retry payment" and "Check Orders"; choosing COD rotated the idempotency key and the bottom Pay button created a second order. (3) A create whose response was lost returned `409 IDEMPOTENT_REPLAY` even though the order existed; the client did not consume `details.order`, so Retry could loop or duplicate. (4) The COD Rs 2,000 cap, `e <= held`, payment-mode normalization, and the 10-jar subscription cap were UI-only or schema-only, so a direct API call could bypass them. The owner approved fix set **A+B+C+E**; **D** (custom dates/twice-daily) is not approved.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Force login on every refresh failure** — rejected: an offline moment or a 503 would sign the customer out; only an explicit auth rejection should end the session.
  2. **Cancel the unpaid UPI order and create a COD replacement** — rejected: it changes the order id the customer already saw, creates a second row under a new key, and risks a delayed UPI capture on the cancelled order.
  3. **Flip `payment_mode` in the client only** — rejected: the server order would still be UPI; the vendor would not know cash is due.
  4. **Let the COD switch re-post create** — rejected: duplicate orders and a second dues post; the approved design converts the existing row in place.
  5. **Keep the cap client-side** — rejected: every money guard must hold against a direct API call.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Session restore** — `AuthController.restoreTimeout` is **20s**, longer than the 12s refresh request. `recoverSession` forces logout only on `ApiException` status **401/403**; network, timeout, 429, 5xx and storage failures return false and leave both stored tokens intact for the next try. The auth gate comment no longer claims a 5s bound.
  2. **Duplicate-create guard** — `_CheckoutSheet` keeps `_placed` after create. `_pay` and `_canPay` refuse while `_placed != null`, payment chips freeze, and recovery only retries the payment step or calls the new COD switch. `_selectSlot` rotates the idempotency key because the window is part of the create payload.
  3. **Replay handling** — `createOnce` catches `IDEMPOTENT_REPLAY` and returns `details.order` as the original row, so the same key continues to the payment step instead of surfacing a 409. A unit test proves one create call, one intent call, the same idempotency key on both, and the original order id.
  4. **COD fallback in place** — new `POST /v1/orders/{id}/switch-to-cod` (owner/user role). `PaymentService.switch_to_cod` re-checks the Rs 2,000 order-total and dues caps, then `PaymentRepo.switch_to_cod` takes `WRITE_LOCK`, rejects cancelled/rejected orders and any captured `paid`/`partial` payment, marks open `link_sent` payments failed, sets `payment_mode='cod'`/`payment_status='unpaid'`, and `cod_confirm` posts dues exactly once. Repeated calls return the COD order; the client shows **Use Cash on Delivery** only in `_Recovery.created`, calls the endpoint, and confirms with `CheckoutResult.asCod()` (`payOnDelivery: true`).
  5. **Server money guards** — `OrderService.create` normalizes `payment_mode` to `upi`/`cod`, rejects `e > ledger.held` (a deposit waiver for empties never handed over), enforces `COD_MAX_PAISE = 200000` on the order total and carried dues, and keeps the hold-block limit at 3. Subscription quantity is capped at **10** in `SubCreateIn`/`SubEstimateIn` and in `SubscriptionService`.
  6. **Honest copy** — the confirm sheet says cash is due at the door for COD; UPI-failure copy names Cash on Delivery; hold-block copy tells the customer to return empties instead of suggesting UPI (which the server also blocks while held > 3); the `COD_NOT_ALLOWED` and `HOLD_BLOCKED` error branches match the server rules.
  7. **Not done** — custom dates (UI allows six, API caps the joined value at 64 chars, 1–5 dates silently become 7 days, `next_run` is never sent) and twice-daily schedules remain blocked until the owner answers the two product questions. No production write, deploy, or real-money test was performed.
- **Why**: The customer must sign in once and stay signed in through transient failures; a paid or placed order must never be duplicated by a retry; and a payment failure must have a real, money-safe fallback. Converting the existing order keeps one order id, one dues event, and one vendor stop. Server-side caps are the only enforcement that survives a direct API call. Only 401/403 proves the session is dead; treating an offline moment as a logout was the actual bug.
- **Consequences**: A slow refresh now holds the splash for up to 20s instead of 5s, which is the correct trade for not signing out a live customer; the gate still falls back to login if the store hangs. A COD switch voids the UPI link, so a delayed capture after the switch is handled by the webhook as a recorded payment/refund path and the vendor cash flow refuses an already-paid order — no double collection. `e > held` changes behavior for callers that returned more empties than the ledger tracked, but the UI always sent the tracked number. Subscription quantity above 10 now returns `VALIDATION` (or 422 at the schema) instead of creating an oversized plan; legacy rows are untouched and the scheduler test seeds qty 11 directly to keep per-plan isolation covered. Custom-date/twice-daily work stays out of scope.
- **Verification**: Backend `.venv\\Scripts\\python.exe -m pytest -q` = **355 passed, 1 skipped** (targeted orders/payments/monthly/scheduler = 48 passed). Flutter `flutter test` = **67/67**, including new transient-session tests and `checkout_service_test.dart` (replay reuses the original order and the same UPI key). `flutter analyze` = No issues found. No production data or Worker deployment changed.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/{api/v1/{payments,subscriptions}.py,repositories/payment_repo.py,services/{order_service,payment_service,subscription_service}.py}`, `workers/api/tests/{test_orders,test_payments,test_monthly_billing,test_scheduler,test_e2e,test_phase01_authz,test_resilience_snapshots}.py`, `apps/user_app_donor/lib/{core/api_client.dart,features/auth/{auth_controller,auth_gate}.dart,features/booking/{booking_sheet,checkout_service,quote_confirm}.dart}`, `apps/user_app_donor/test/{auth_password,checkout_service}_test.dart`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-137: Cloudflare agent setup — official skills, remote MCP OAuth (2026-10-25)
- **Date**: 2026-10-25
- **Status**: Accepted (environment setup verified with `opencode mcp list`; context docs committed locally on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; not pushed)
- **Context**: After the production closeout, the owner completed `wrangler login` and asked to execute the official setup at `https://developers.cloudflare.com/agent-setup/prompt.md`. That prompt requires installing the Cloudflare skills and registering the Cloudflare MCP server, with the `cf` CLI explicitly optional. This project already deploys with Wrangler and D1; the setup was not meant to change app code or production data.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Install only the already-present `agents-sdk` skill** - rejected: the official prompt asks for the Cloudflare skill pack, and the repository ships a current 19-skill set covering Wrangler, Workers, D1-adjacent products, performance, and security workflows.
  2. **Register the MCP server with a static API-token header** - rejected: the remote server advertises OAuth, and OAuth avoids pasting a durable account token into the global config. The refresh token stays in OpenCode's local auth store outside the repo.
  3. **Install the optional `cf` CLI as well** - deferred: the prompt marks it optional, Wrangler is already installed and is this repo's deploy path, and adding another global binary was not needed to complete the requested setup.
  4. **Choose Minimal or Read-only scopes at consent** - the completed consent returned **Full access** scopes. That is recorded rather than hidden; re-running the flow solely to downscope would require another browser consent and could break a working connection. The owner can run `opencode mcp logout cloudflare` and re-authenticate with a narrower scope if least privilege is preferred.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Install the full skill pack globally** - `npx -y skills add cloudflare/skills --skill '*' --yes --global` installed 19 Cloudflare skills under `C:\Users\Hp\.agents\skills`: agents-sdk, basin, cf-cli, cloudflare, cloudflare-email-service, cloudflare-one, cloudflare-one-migrations, durable-objects, gateway-workers, k2, nextjs-on-cloudflare, sandbox-migrate-to-next, sandbox-next, sandbox-stable, turnstile-spin, web-perf, workers-best-practices, workers-profiling, and wrangler.
  2. **Register the remote MCP server** - added `mcp.cloudflare` to `C:\Users\Hp\.config\opencode\opencode.jsonc` with `type: "remote"`, `url: "https://mcp.cloudflare.com/mcp"`, `enabled: true`, and `oauth: {}`. Existing provider configuration and keys were preserved untouched.
  3. **Complete OAuth** - ran `opencode mcp auth cloudflare` from a detached process so a server restart would not kill the callback. The flow completed in the system browser; `opencode mcp list` reports `cloudflare connected (OAuth)`. The token is stored at `C:\Users\Hp\.local\share\opencode\mcp-auth.json`; no token or refresh token was printed, copied, or committed.
  4. **Record the actual scope** - the local token metadata shows `offline_access user:read account:read` plus broad read/write scopes (Full access). The token expires at `2026-10-25T06:29:22Z` and a refresh token is present.
  5. **Leave production alone** - no D1 write, Worker deploy, or secret change was made by this setup. Secret rotation remains an owner-only action, and the optional `cf` CLI was not installed.
- **Why**: The official Cloudflare prompt is the source of truth for this setup, and OAuth is the intended path for the hosted MCP server. Global installation matches how the user asked to be set up, while leaving the repo free of generated skill copies. Using OAuth instead of a static account API token avoids embedding a long-lived Cloudflare credential in the global config. The scope gap is surfaced explicitly so the owner can tighten it if desired.
- **Consequences**: New OpenCode sessions load the Cloudflare MCP tools and all 19 skills; an already-running session may need a restart before the MCP tools appear. The OAuth grant is broad (Full access), so the token should be treated as a sensitive credential; it lives outside the repo and can be revoked with `opencode mcp logout cloudflare`. The first `opencode mcp list` after the auth timed out once, then returned `connected (OAuth)` on retry; `opencode mcp debug cloudflare` also reported a successful connection. The `cf` CLI remains uninstalled and Wrangler remains the project deploy path.
- **Verification**: `C:\Users\Hp\.agents\skills` contains 19 Cloudflare skill directories; `C:\Users\Hp\.config\opencode\opencode.jsonc` parses as JSON and exposes only `mcp.cloudflare` under `mcp` while retaining the existing `provider` block; `opencode mcp list` = connected (OAuth); `opencode mcp debug cloudflare` = "Connection successful (already authenticated)"; no token value appeared in chat or the repo. Production counts and the live Worker were not touched during this setup.
- **Affects**: `C:\Users\Hp\.config\opencode\opencode.jsonc`, `C:\Users\Hp\.agents\skills`, `C:\Users\Hp\.local\share\opencode\mcp-auth.json`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-136: Production closeout executed — QA data removed, demo door closed (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Accepted (production cleanup and demo shutdown executed and verified; committed locally on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; not pushed; secret rotation remains owner-only)
- **Context**: ADR-135 prepared the cleanup and demo-shutdown scripts but could not run them because `wrangler` was unauthenticated. The owner then completed `wrangler login` (OAuth session for the owner account), which unblocked D1 writes. Before deleting anything, a fresh production export was taken and the nine allowlisted ids were checked. That pre-flight found only **eight** matches: the list contained `1f28f492a81f466a8264b39adb24084e`, while the real user id is `1f28b492a81f466a8264b39adb24084e`. The miss was not cosmetic — it owned the postpaid order `2236358550c8425abd9a23f1af39839e`, so running the script unchanged would have left one QA user, the order, and its subscription behind.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Run the prepared script as-is** — rejected: the final count would be 10, and the postpaid QA order (the one the earlier live-PoD discussion referenced) would survive the cleanup.
  2. **Broaden the delete to a phone/name pattern** — rejected: production also has real customers; the explicit allowlist is the safety boundary.
  3. **Correct the id, dry-run on the exact production backup, then execute** — chosen: the only path that both removes the intended rows and proves the result before the destructive write.
  4. **Skip the remote cleanup and leave the runbook** — rejected: the owner explicitly asked to close what was still open, and the authenticated session made execution possible.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Correct the allowlist** — `1f28f492…` was replaced with `1f28b492…` in the cleanup script's delete allowlist and verification query, and in every context-doc reference.
  2. **Dry-run on the fresh production backup** — the corrected script was executed twice against `shodasha_pre_closeout_20261024.sql` (18 users) before the remote run: users **18 → 9**, QA ids **0**, postpaid order **0**, `demo-user-1`/`demo-vendor-1`/admin intact, demo flag **0**, demo codes **0**, `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` empty.
  3. **Execute remotely** — `npx wrangler d1 execute shodasha --remote --file scripts/cleanup_qa_20261024.sql` processed **38 queries**, wrote **102 rows**, and returned bookmark `00000067-00000008-0000510e-2e3845f14a69595236d7a248c9d0a50f`. `scripts/disable_demo_login.sql` processed **3 queries**, wrote **5 rows**, and returned bookmark `00000067-0000000e-0000510e-46aa8c2ac4185bba11c85216008e18c6`.
  4. **Verify at the data and HTTP doors** — final users **9**, QA ids **0**, `demo-user-1`/`demo-vendor-1`/admin present, postpaid order **0**, orders **9**, subscriptions **0**, refunds **1**, dues intents **0**, subscription charges **0**, payments **5**, `demo_login_enabled='0'`, `demo_codes=0`, `foreign_key_check` **0 violations**, `demo-user-1` dues `26400` untouched, live `POST /v1/auth/demo` **401**, `GET /v1/catalog` **200**.
  5. **Leave rotation to the owner** — the OAuth session can write Workers/D1 but cannot regenerate the Cloudflare API tokens, R2 keys, or Razorpay secrets. No secret value was created, stored, or printed in this execution.
- **Why**: A destructive production script must be proven against the exact data it will delete. The pre-flight caught a one-character scope bug that would have left the postpaid QA order alive and made the "9 users" verification fail. Correcting it, re-running the dry-run on the fresh backup, and then executing in one verified sequence is the only acceptable order. The final HTTP checks matter because a database count alone does not prove the demo door is closed or the Worker still serves traffic.
- **Consequences**: Production now has 9 users: the admin, `demo-user-1`, `demo-vendor-1`, and six pre-existing real accounts. All nine QA users and their orders/subscriptions/refunds/dues/charges/payments are gone; the postpaid test order is deleted with its owner, so no separate live PoD is owed. The demo accounts remain for local seeding, but the production demo login is closed (flag 0, codes deleted, live 401). Secret rotation is still pending and remains the only open security action. No push or merge occurred.
- **Verification**: Backup `C:\Users\Hp\AppData\Local\Temp\opencode\shodasha_pre_closeout_20261024.sql`, 140,716 bytes, SHA256 `A7C99F21C52C4CD3228A608C7A8E5C78E57E01FF7F4AFEB9871A632BE50A120D`. Pre-flight before correction: 8/9 ids, all `role='user'`; after correction: 9/9. Remote bookmarks above. Final query set: users 9, qa_left 0, admin/demo rows present, postpaid order 0, demo flag 0, demo codes 0, orders 9, subscriptions 0, refunds 1, dues intents 0, subscription charges 0, payments 5, `fk_violations` 0, `demo-user-1` dues 26400. Live checks: demo login 401, catalog 200.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/scripts/cleanup_qa_20261024.sql`, `workers/api/scripts/disable_demo_login.sql`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, production D1 `shodasha` (`da762aad-1d5c-46eb-992e-acc79505cc95`), branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-135: Money-path closeout — real PoD route regression, QA cleanup, and demo-door shutdown (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Accepted (code, scripts and docs on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; production cleanup, demo-door shutdown and secret rotation remain owner actions because `wrangler` is unauthenticated; not pushed)
- **Context**: ADR-134 shipped and live-verified the money fixes, but three release leftovers remained. First, the postpaid dues-on-delivery rule was covered at the service/repository level, not through the HTTP door a rider actually calls, and the cron-generated postpaid order had no vendor route or delivery code, so a live PoD was not possible. Second, the ADR-133/ADR-134 QA users, orders, payments, subscriptions, refunds and sessions were still in production; ADR-133 explicitly deferred their deletion until the owner confirmed scope. Third, the production demo door was still open: `config.demo_login_enabled=1` and matching `demo_codes` rows let the seeded test vendor/customer sign in with published codes. `npx wrangler whoami` returned "You are not authenticated", no `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` was present, and the Cloudflare dashboard redirected to `/login`, so no D1 write, cleanup, or secret rotation was possible from this session.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Chase a live production PoD on the cron order** — rejected: the order is `placed` for QA user `1f28b492a81f466a8264b39adb24084e` (one of the rows to delete), no route/stop or delivery code exists, and the demo vendor has no today route, no zone pool, and cannot accept it. Manufacturing route/stop/session rows in production would be riskier than the coverage it buys.
  2. **Delete QA rows by name/phone pattern** — rejected: production also has real customers, and a pattern can match them. An explicit id allowlist is auditable and bounded.
  3. **Close the demo door by setting the flag only** — rejected: the published code hashes would stay in `demo_codes`, so a later flag flip would reopen the same credentials. Flag 0 plus deleting every code is strictly safer.
  4. **Run the production scripts without confirmed access** — rejected: blind or unauthenticated D1 mutation is unsafe and would make the outcome unverifiable.
  5. **Chosen: add a real HTTP PoD regression, write two idempotent FK-safe self-verifying SQL scripts, dry-run both twice on the pre-028 backup plus migration 028, and record the exact owner runbook and rotation list without claiming production cleanup.**
- **Decision**:
  1. **Real HTTP PoD regression** — `test_real_pod_route_posts_postpaid_dues_once` seeds a vendor user, a `monthly_postpaid` subscription, a real order transitioned through to `dispatched`, a route/stop with a live delivery code and a vendor session, then calls `POST /v1/vendor/stops/s1/pod` through the real FastAPI router. It asserts the stop is `done`, `ledger.dues` equals the order total, and a second PoD call returns `replay: true` without changing dues. The test harness now applies migrations `007`, `010`, `015` and `027` so the route, audit/config and delivery-code tables exist.
  2. **QA cleanup by explicit allowlist** — `workers/api/scripts/cleanup_qa_20261024.sql` holds the nine disposable customer ids, hard-aborts before any delete if an existing allowlisted id is not `role='user'` (a `CHECK` guard; missing ids are fine for idempotent re-runs), deletes FK children in dependency order, removes phone-keyed non-FK rows (`otp_codes`, `demo_codes`, `leads`), deletes the users only when `role='user'`, retains `audit_log` as the security trail, and prints `remaining_qa`, `users_total` and `demo_user`. It never uses a name or phone pattern and never includes `demo-user-1`, `demo-vendor-1`, the admin, or a pre-existing customer.
  3. **Demo-door shutdown** — `workers/api/scripts/disable_demo_login.sql` sets `config.demo_login_enabled='0'` (insert-or-replace), deletes every `demo_codes` row, and verifies both values. The demo accounts stay for local QA; only the production login path closes.
  4. **Disposable postpaid order** — live delivery of `2236358550c8425abd9a23f1af39839e` is intentionally not chased. It belongs to a QA user in the cleanup allowlist, so the correct closeout is to delete it with that user; the dues-on-delivery route is now regression-locked at the real HTTP door. If the owner wants a live production PoD after cleanup, it should be done on a real, non-QA postpaid order with the assigned vendor and its delivery code.
  5. **Owner runbook** — after `npx wrangler login` in `workers/api`: export a fresh D1 backup, run the cleanup script, run the demo shutdown script, then verify users = 9, demo flag = 0, demo codes = 0, and `POST /v1/auth/demo` non-200. Both scripts are idempotent and were run twice locally.
  6. **Secret rotation** — the two pasted Cloudflare API tokens (deploy/tail and D1), the R2 access key/secret, and the Razorpay key secret and webhook secret are all treated as exposed. The owner rotates them in the respective dashboards, updates Worker secrets `UPI_KEY_SECRET` and `UPI_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, and never writes the new values to the repo or chat. No secret value was stored in this work.
- **Why**: The release's central promise is that a delivered postpaid order creates dues exactly once. Testing that at the same HTTP door the rider uses is stronger than a repository-level test and does not require touching a live QA order. Cleanup must be narrow enough that a real customer cannot be caught by a pattern; an explicit id list plus a role pre-flight is the only acceptable shape for production deletion. Closing both demo gates prevents a future operator from reopening the published credentials by flipping one flag. Finally, without authenticated access the honest state is "scripts ready, production unchanged", not a claim that the cleanup happened.
- **Consequences**: Production still has 18 users, the QA rows, and an enabled demo door until the owner runs the two scripts. The postpaid order remains `placed` for a QA user and will be deleted with that user. Re-running either script is safe. Secret rotation invalidates the old credentials immediately; any local `.env` copy (gitignored) must be updated or discarded separately. The demo accounts remain in D1 for local seeding, so local QA is unaffected after the production door closes. No real money moved, and nothing was pushed or merged.
- **Verification**: `workers/api` → `uv run pytest -q` **350 passed / 1 skipped** (`test_p0_money.py` now 10). Local closeout dry-run: pre-028 backup + migration 028, each script executed twice → backup users **10 → 9**, QA ids **0**, `demo-user-1`/`demo-vendor-1`/admin **1** each, `demo_login_enabled` **0**, `demo_codes` **0**, `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` **empty**. A simulated role change to `vendor` for the ADR-133 QA id aborted the cleanup with `CHECK constraint failed` **before** deleting that user's order or user row. Live production evidence for the exposure, not a fix: demo vendor login with the seeded demo credentials succeeded; `/v1/vendor/routes/today` returned no route; `/v1/vendor/placed` returned an empty pool; demo customer login succeeded; `demo-order-1` is `dispatched` with `delivery_otp_state=unavailable`. `npx wrangler whoami` remained unauthenticated, so no production cleanup or rotation was attempted.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/tests/test_p0_money.py`, `workers/api/scripts/cleanup_qa_20261024.sql`, `workers/api/scripts/disable_demo_login.sql`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`; production D1 and secrets are unchanged.

### ADR-134: P0 money fixes + real monthly billing — honest cancellation, late-capture refunds, prepaid credit ledger (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Accepted (committed `f0c3ee5`, `fdc125b`, `62828fc`, `3e88eb6` on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; migration 028 applied to production D1 and Worker deployed as `511879dc-60d4-48a2-aca9-7ae44a734f8a`; not pushed)
- **Context**: ADR-133 proved five release-blocking money defects: dues UPI 500'd on the `payments.order_id` FK after creating a provider order; canceled unpaid orders stayed payable/countable and a late webhook could settle them; cancellation wrote off dues and invented refunds with zero collected money; production cron never created subscription orders; a same-key intent retry minted a second provider link and returned a reference absent from D1. The owner approved fixing all five, making monthly billing real rather than a stored label, and replacing refund promises the app cannot keep.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Patch only the symptoms** (relax the FK, block webhooks by status, hide the monthly chips) — rejected: the schema would still have no way to identify a dues intent or a prepaid credit movement, so retries, refunds, and reconciliation would stay guesswork.
  2. **Keep monthly as a label and collect nothing until a future release** — rejected: the product would advertise a billing mode it does not bill; the owner explicitly required actual charge/credit consumption.
  3. **Chosen: additive migration with dedicated money tables + a rebuilt refunds table, provider calls only after durable reservations, and a server-computed prepaid credit ledger.**
- **Decision**:
  1. **Dues no longer abuse `payments`** — migration `028_p0_money_monthly.sql` adds `dues_intents` (user, amount, provider ref/link, status) and `subscription_charges` (one prepaid top-up per subscription). `POST /payments/dues-intent` reserves the intent first, calls the provider second, and attaches the provider reference third; a same-key replay returns the stored intent/link and never calls the provider again. Settlement allocates to open orders, marks each one `paid_upi`/`partial_dues`, and — when that order's rupees already sit in `ledger.dues` — reduces the ledger once, so the door cannot collect the same money again.
  2. **Canceled means unpayable** — cancel voids open payment links and marks the order unpaid-canceled; the webhook refuses to settle a canceled/rejected order. If money was already captured (late capture), the payment is recorded as captured, a pending refund is queued for the captured amount, and the order is **never** marked paid. `cancel_settle` commits its ledger/refund work as one unit.
  3. **Cancellation stops inventing money** — subscription cancel refunds only collected prepaid credit (recomputed from `SUM(subscription_credit_entries.delta)`), never writes off ledger dues, and prorates against the real scheduler idempotency key prefix. Jar deposits stay tied to pickup.
  4. **Production cron is real** — `entry.py` calls `cron_tick(D1Conn(env.DB))` on the scheduled trigger. The Cloudflare runtime passed `env=None` to `scheduled` (live `wrangler tail` showed `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'DB'`), so the handler now reads bindings from `self.env` — the `WorkerEntrypoint` contract — and keeps the argument only as a fallback. A live 14:30 UTC tick generated the due order and advanced `next_run`.
  5. **Monthly prepaid** — UPI only. At activation the user pays `qty × rate × 30` (server-computed water estimate; deposit excluded because it is refundable and collected on first delivery). Each scheduled delivery debits its water bill from `subscription_credit_entries`; orders use `payment_mode='upi'`, `payment_status='paid_upi'` (or `partial_dues` when the balance cannot cover the whole bill, with the remainder on the ledger), and a synthetic `subcredit:<sub_id>:<order_id>` payment row. Insufficient balance pauses generation and returns a `low_balance` item instead of overdrawing; cancel refunds the remaining balance.
  6. **Monthly postpaid** — no upfront charge. A scheduled order stays unpaid until delivery completes; on the `delivered` transition `OrderRepo` recognises the scheduler key `POST /v1/orders:sub:<sub_id>:<date>`, confirms the plan is `monthly_postpaid` for the same user, and posts `total − paid` as real ledger dues with an idempotent `order:<id>` event. One monthly UPI dues payment then clears them. An order canceled before delivery posts nothing, so an auto-generated order that never arrives cannot create debt. Monthly modes reject COD at creation.
  7. **Refund destination persists** — `subscriptions.refund_upi` and `refunds.upi_id` store the cancel-time UPI so a prepaid balance refund is actionable even when there is no jar return to carry the address. `refunds.order_id` becomes nullable and `subscription_id` is added, so a subscription refund can exist without an order.
  8. **Honest copy** — because there is no customer-facing refund list, cancel copy says a due refund is processed to the supplied UPI after pickup. It never says the refund will "appear in Wallet" or is "on its way", and it states that nothing is refunded for deliveries never paid.
- **Why**: Money invariants must hold before UI claims: a provider order may never exist without a D1 row that can settle/refund it; a canceled order may never take money; cancellation may never erase dues or promise uncollected money; and a prepaid plan must actually consume a balance, otherwise "monthly prepaid" is a lie. Dedicated tables keep order payments and subscription credit separable, which is what makes replay, refund, and audit deterministic. The `status='paused'` + `canceled_at` representation is retained because the existing CHECK constraint allows only `active|paused`; `billing_status` carries the payment lifecycle instead of weakening the constraint in a hurry.
- **Consequences**: Migration 028 must be applied before the Worker deploy (new tables/columns are required). Monthly plans are UPI-only and need a real provider confirmation before deliveries start (`billing_status='pending_payment'` until the webhook settles). Prepaid refunds depend on the user supplying a UPI at cancel; without it the refund row still exists but is not payable. The customer app cannot list refunds, so copy stays process-oriented. Local SQLite tests now enable `PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON` so the original P0-1 class is caught.
- **Verification**: `workers/api` → `python -m pytest -q` **349 passed / 1 skipped**; `apps/user_app_donor` → `dart format` clean, `flutter analyze` no issues, `flutter test` **63/63**. New regressions: `tests/test_p0_money.py` (9, FK-on; dues intent replay/provider-once + order close, canceled-order webhook refund, cancel with zero payments, prepaid debit/pause/refund with UPI, cron idempotency, postpaid delivery accrues dues once + dues payment closes the order, postpaid cancel-before-delivery owes nothing) and `test/subscription_billing_test.dart` (4; billing parse, UPI-only monthly creation, awaiting-payment/top-up card, honest cancel copy). Live production evidence (no real money moved): migration 028 applied; Worker `c0134982` → `a3adb6a7` → `649f7b82` → `511879dc` deployed; prepaid create → ₹840 top-up (`order_TrlciejmvgSl3q`) → same-key replay → cancel with honest empty refund; signed webhook settled a second ₹840 top-up (`order_Trmb9slLlJgdLm`) to `active`, replay kept the balance unchanged, and cancel queued an ₹840 refund; COD dues ₹28 survived cancel and the dues intent returned `order_TrleXAcN29Oeke`; paying ₹28 by signed webhook cleared dues to 0 **and** marked the COD order `paid_upi`; a late capture on a canceled UPI order (`order_TrmfQs9IHHEzTt`) left the order `cancelled`/`unpaid`, recorded the payment, and queued a ₹28 pending refund; the 14:30 UTC cron tick logged `outcome=ok` and generated the postpaid test order.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/db/migrations/028_p0_money_monthly.sql`, `workers/api/src/app/{services/{payment_service,subscription_service},repositories/{payment_repo,order_repo,admin_read_repo},jobs/scheduler.py,entry.py,api/v1/payments.py}`, `workers/api/tests/test_p0_money.py`, `apps/user_app_donor/lib/{core/api_client.dart,features/subscriptions/*}`, `apps/user_app_donor/test/subscription_billing_test.dart`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`.

### ADR-133: Auth, UPI, subscription and vendor-flow audit — five release-blocking money defects (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Recorded (audit only; no application code, schema, production data, deploy or push changed; D1 cleanup pending)
- **Context**: The 035 release candidate now points at the production Worker, and auth had just been connected. Before promising subscriptions or dues-UPI, the owner asked for an evidence-based audit of customer auth, UPI payment/webhook, subscription, and vendor-update flows. The audit had to use live requests plus a disposable local reproduction without touching real users or real money.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Code review only** — rejected: the owner required status/trace or executable evidence for every scenario.
  2. **Exercise everything in production** — rejected: would create money/order state and risk real users; the safe path was one disposable QA user plus local SQLite for destructive cases.
  3. **Chosen: live read/auth/webhook-fail-closed calls on one QA user, then stop after the first P0, and reproduce money/subscription paths against disposable SQLite with the real services/repos and a stubbed Razorpay HTTP boundary.**
- **Decision**:
  1. **Live auth matrix** — register 200, duplicate 409 `ACCOUNT_EXISTS`, login 200, wrong password 401 (generic `UNAUTH`, not the spec's `INVALID_CREDENTIALS`), refresh rotation 200, old-refresh reuse 401 with rotated-access revocation, logout 200, post-logout `/auth/me` 401. All recorded with trace ids.
  2. **Live money smoke** — address 201, quote 200 (₹56), UPI order 201, UPI intent 201 (Razorpay test order + `upi://` link), unsigned and forged webhooks 401. The first `POST /payments/dues-intent` returned 500 (trace `6566a78274c34563`), which triggered the stop rule.
  3. **Local reproduction** — disposable SQLite with migrations 002-027, real services/repositories, stubbed Razorpay. Confirmed: dues intent fails with `PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON`; two active plans can coexist; one-time orders are allowed during a plan; `monthly_prepaid` creates zero payments; cancel wipes ₹56 dues and claims ₹1,624 with zero payments; proration lookup misses the `POST /v1/orders:sub:` key; a canceled `link_sent` order is settled by a captured webhook with no refund; a same-key intent retry calls the provider twice and returns a ref absent from `payments`.
  4. **Report** — five P0s (dues FK/provider-first, canceled-order payability/webhook settlement, cancel write-off/invented refunds, production cron no-op, intent retry orphan link), six P1s, six P2s, subscription/vendor deep dives, exact UX copy, blocked tests, cleanup state, and evidence index in `Feature_docs/platform-audit/2026-10-24-auth-upi-subscriptions-vendor-audit.md`.
  5. **Cleanup boundary** — the QA address was deleted and the QA order canceled/logged out, but the canceled order remains `payment_status=link_sent` and the user/payment/session/idempotency/event rows remain. `wrangler` was not authenticated, so no D1 delete or user-count verification was possible. No owner phone, existing user, password guess, rate-limit hammering, or real-money payment was used.
- **Why**: Auth and webhook signature handling are healthy, but the money layer fails on invariants that matter more than UI polish: never take money for a canceled order, never erase dues without collected money, never promise a refund that was never collected, and never create a provider link the backend cannot settle. The five P0s are release blockers, not backlog items. Local reproduction was necessary because continuing in production after the first P0 would have created more unrecoverable QA state.
- **Consequences**: The release remains do-not-ship for subscriptions/dues-UPI until P0-1 through P0-5 are fixed. The current 34-test payment/subscription slice is green but does not enable SQLite foreign keys, so it cannot catch the dues FK class. The QA user (`69af983e97af40f2aeb4e2c2f2b4ba33`), payment `051007dd27ef4080a0d59b0daa35b20c`, canceled order `110cb098df6841fb9a6aa6b4424cf287`, and Razorpay test order `order_Trg9yamrg2L9fm` remain for owner cleanup; production count is expected to be 10 but unverified. Test-mode keys only, so no real money moved. The report recommends a fix order and exact customer/vendor copy; no fix was implemented in this audit.
- **Verification**: Live traces (`54cefc4b1a654439`, `d5a462beccc84427`, `cbc422b4fa994004`, `2671a644c2154c55`, `2ccbb319dfa94c9d`, `6566a78274c34563`, `ab304517904c4818`, `2bf72ca7a16e49d1`), local outputs (`local-sub.out.txt`, `local-cancel-dues.out.txt`, `local-cancel-webhook.out.txt`, `local-proration.out.txt`, `local-webhook.out.txt`, `local-intent-idem.out.txt`, `repro-dues-fk.out.txt`), and targeted pytest **34 passed**. Evidence directory: `%TEMP%\shodasha-audit-20261024` (tokens revoked/redacted).
- **Affects**: `Feature_docs/platform-audit/2026-10-24-auth-upi-subscriptions-vendor-audit.md`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`; no source files changed.

### ADR-132: Production Cloudflare auth connection — D1 migration repair and PBKDF2 runtime cap (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Accepted (production deploy authorized and completed; committed locally on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; no push)
- **Context**: The 035 release APK already embeds the production Worker URL, but the live Worker predated 034 customer auth (`POST /v1/auth/user/login` returned 404), and production registration returned 500 after the route was deployed. The live D1 schema was at 023 with an empty `d1_migrations` table, so migrations 024-027 (password columns and delivery-code lifecycle) had never been applied. The Pyodide password path also used a missing `to_py` import and JS/Python conversions that only fail in the Workers runtime. Cloudflare's WebCrypto then rejected the spec's 600,000-iteration PBKDF2 call outright.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Leave production at schema 023 / no customer auth** — rejected: the release APK cannot sign in and the connection task is unfinished.
  2. **Rebuild the database from migrations** — rejected: would destroy production data; a backup plus forward migrations is the safe path.
  3. **Keep 600k and move hashing to an external service or WASM** — rejected for now: outside 034 scope, larger architecture change; the owner chose the working in-runtime path.
  4. **Chosen: back up D1, backfill migration bookkeeping, apply 024-027, fix the Pyodide bindings, and use an explicit 100,000-iteration PBKDF2 parameter** — the only path that makes the existing Worker auth work without changing the auth design.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Backup first** — exported production D1 to `C:\Users\Hp\AppData\Local\Temp\shodasha-prod-backup-20261024.sql` (95,291 bytes, SHA256 `F3726161FB9A9C5B340C92F9F35C84C74684BA2CACB8904704BE31062E465905`). The backup is outside the repo and is not committed.
  2. **Migration bookkeeping** — the live schema was at 023 but `d1_migrations` was empty. Backfilled `002`-`023` as already applied, then applied `024`-`027`; the live database now records 26 migrations. An explicit `BEGIN` failed with `{"D1_RESET_DO":true}`; the retry without an explicit transaction succeeded.
  3. **Worker deploy** — deployed the current Worker; version `07e98028-2434-4487-aad1-be52938f97cb` serves `https://water.adityathakur452007.workers.dev`. No app-side URL change was needed because the APK already embeds this URL.
  4. **Pyodide password fixes** — removed the missing `to_py` import; use `bits.to_py()` for the derived bits; convert the usages list with `to_js([...])`; convert the PBKDF2 algorithm object with `Object.fromEntries`. These are runtime-binding fixes, not algorithm changes.
  5. **Explicit 100k PBKDF2 cap** — Cloudflare WebCrypto returns `NotSupportedError: Pbkdf2 failed: iteration counts above 100000 are not supported (requested 600000)`. The owner explicitly approved `ITERATIONS = 100_000`; the dummy timing hash was regenerated at 100k and the test assertion updated to `pbkdf2_sha256$100000$`. This is a documented runtime maximum, not a silent fallback or a runtime-selectable cost.
  6. **Live verification** — `GET /v1/catalog` 200; `POST /v1/auth/user/login` with `{}` 400 VALIDATION; registration with a temporary account 200; login 200; wrong password 401 with generic `UNAUTH`; unknown phone 401 through the 100k dummy hash (no 500/enumeration). The temporary user (`529e5660063044a7b3b23d7ed4a37b82`), its 2 sessions and 2 phone rate-counter rows were deleted; production user count is back to 9 and the test phones are absent.
  7. **Guardrails preserved** — no push, no merge to `main`, no real-user migration. The APK remains debug-signed. Existing production users all have empty `password_hash` values and cannot sign in until an admin password reset or a new registration.
- **Why**: The app was already pointed at production; leaving the Worker at 023 meant the release could never authenticate. Backfilling bookkeeping instead of rebuilding preserves real data. The Pyodide fixes are narrow and testable. The 100k parameter is the only PBKDF2 cost the runtime accepts; making it explicit and regenerating the dummy hash avoids a 500 on unknown-phone timing and keeps the security tradeoff visible. The alternative — an external auth service — is a larger product decision, not a deployment fix.
- **Consequences**: 100,000 iterations is below the original 600,000 target and OWASP's PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 guidance; the mitigation is the existing rate limits, minimum password policy, generic auth errors and no secret logging. Any future stored hash above 100k cannot be verified on this Worker and would need a reset or a different runtime. The existing 9 users are locked out until an admin reset; that is intentional fail-closed behavior for empty hashes, not a regression. The release APK is still Android Debug-signed, and physical-device smoke remains owner work. Cloudflare tokens were pasted into chat and are not stored in the repo; rotate them. The R2 credentials were not used and should also be rotated. `GET /v1/health` is not a valid readiness check because no health route exists; use `/v1/catalog` or auth validation.
- **Verification**: backend `uv run pytest -q` = **337 passed, 1 skipped**; live catalog/login/registration/wrong-password/unknown-phone checks above; test user cleanup verified with user count 9, test phone 0, test sessions 0, test rate rows 0. The APK was built before the backend fixes; no app code changed, so its embedded production URL remains valid.
- **Affects**: `workers/api/src/app/core/passwords.py`, `workers/api/tests/test_access_code_auth.py`, `Feature_docs/user-app-donor/spec-034-auth-password-delivery-otp.md`, `context/{progress-tracker,flow,decision}.md`, Cloudflare Worker `water`, D1 `shodasha`, branch `035-premium-ui-states-release`

### ADR-131: 035 WS2–WS5 — premium screen migration, state truth, and release verification (2026-10-24)
- **Date**: 2026-10-24
- **Status**: Accepted (committed locally on `035-premium-ui-states-release`; not pushed; owner deploy/keystore/device smoke open)
- **Context**: WS1 built the shared foundation and WS6–WS8 built the engineering lane, but the donor customer screens still carried the audit's P0/P1 state gaps: inconsistent CTA/card grammar, mixed old and new tokens, missing empty/error/stale branches, unsafe checkout/address states, order-card money copy that could lie, subscription copy that implied an online payment, hidden dues/returns actions, and support/tracking dead ends. The owner approved the full 035 scope on 2026-10-23, including all P0/P1 findings, targeted P2 polish, screen splits, CI, and the 2-ABI size gate.
- **Options considered**:
  1. **Restyle screens in place without the shared primitives** — rejected: would preserve the two-token drift and repeat the same state bugs.
  2. **Rewrite whole screens** — rejected by the approved spec: preserve donor grammar and flows; split only where files were oversized.
  3. **Chosen: migrate each workstream onto `ShodashaTheme` + `lib/shared/ui/`, add explicit state branches, and keep every money/booking call server-authoritative** — smallest diff that closes the audit without new dependencies or backend changes.
- **Decision**:
  1. **Screen grammar** — every donor screen now uses `AppButton`, `AppIconButton`, `AppCard`, `AppSheet`, `AppStateView`, `AppBanner`, `StatusPill`, `SectionHeader`, and `AppTextField`; screen-local hex and one-off state widgets were removed. Green remains money-only; teal remains structure/navigation; solar remains badges/highlights.
  2. **Auth/home/shell** — tokenized login and address states; local-first logout; demo sheet retained as server-gated QA; truthful “Pick a 30-min slot” copy; mode cards use ink ripples; stale/offline banners; M3 navigation; notification/profile/search/support wiring; `Plans` is the bottom-nav label so it fits at 360dp (screen title remains `Subscriptions`).
  3. **Orders/tracking/returns** — `DonorOrderCard` rewritten with sentence-case statuses, `View bill` + `View details` for closed cards, `View bill` + `Get help` for rejected/failed; no secret delivery code in list cards; tracking shows server code states, reschedule/cancel recovery, background poll pause/resume; returns validate VPA and map 401 to a sign-in action.
  4. **Booking/addresses/subscriptions** — slots only from `GET /windows` (never a fabricated `08:00` or `Morning 8-12` fallback); quote invalidation; 401/403 “Sign in again”; Add-address CTA; map-pin coordinate retention; serviceability/dirty guards; plan cap 10 + submit lock; subscription creation copy says plan created/no charge collected, never “Order confirmed” or “first cycle paid online”; dues launch the returned UPI deep link with Copy link + Wallet fallback.
  5. **Wallet/profile/support** — stale ledger retained and labelled; dues above the hero; return action hidden when held is zero; receipts and payment history; profile dirty/save guards; support complaint picker/validation/deadline/stale retention; expired-window recovery offers both Call and WhatsApp.
  6. **Copy decisions** — user-facing order statuses/buttons use sentence case; delivery-code heading is English only (`Delivery code`); password hint is shortened to `8+ characters, letter + number` to avoid truncation at 360dp.
  7. **Engineering** — no new packages; `flutter_animate` removed because the decorative image motion was replaced with `TweenAnimationBuilder`; catalog/windows remain the only cached reads; no backend files changed.
